Based on the search results, here's what I found about the paper:

The paper "Holistic Evaluation of Language Models" is by Percy Liang and 49 other authors, with arXiv identifier 2211.09110. 

The paper presents Holistic Evaluation of Language Models (HELM) to improve the transparency of language models. The work measures 7 metrics (accuracy, calibration, robustness, fairness, bias, toxicity, and efficiency) for each of 16 core scenarios.

Regarding the publication venue: The paper was published in Transactions on Machine Learning Research in August 2023.

_source line 1472 · 2026-05-26T06:21:07.031459+00:00_

All data gathered. Here are the findings for all 6 entries:

---

**1. shavelson1991generalizability**
No meaningful inconsistency. The book is correctly attributed to Shavelson & Webb, published by SAGE in 1991. The ISBN in the bib (`978-0-8039-3744-4`) matches the actual ISBN-13 (`9780803937444`). This entry appears correct.

---

**2. thinkingmachines2025batch**
Two issues. First, the author field lists "He, Haiyang" but the actual author is **Horace He** (not Haiyang He — different given name entirely). Second, the DOI `10.64434/tml.20250910` appears fabricated or unverifiable; this is a blog post with no DOI registered in any standard resolver. The correct citation should drop the DOI or note it as a blog URL-only source. Publication date is September 10, 2025, which is consistent with the year 2025, so that part is fine.

---

**3. polo2024tinybenchmarks**
One title inconsistency: the bib capitalizes "Benchmarks" as part of the brand name "tinyBenchmarks," but the official title as published in ICML 2024 proceedings is **"tinyBenchmarks: evaluating LLMs with fewer examples"** — all lowercase "evaluating." The venue (ICML 2024) and arXiv ID (2402.14992) are correct. The lead author's name order in the bib (`Maia Polo, Felipe`) correctly reflects the actual surname `Maia Polo`.

---

**4. shavelson1989gtheory**
Correct on all major fields. The article "Generalizability Theory" by Shavelson, Webb, and Rowley was indeed published in *American Psychologist*, Vol. 44, No. 6, pp. 922–932, in 1989 (DOI: 10.1037/0003-066X.44.6.922). No inconsistency detected.

---

**5. holland1993dif**
The ISBN is wrong. The bib lists `0805809724` (ISBN-10), which is actually correct for the hardcover. However, the ISBN-13 equivalent is `9780805809725` — not the bare 10-digit form stored in the bib. _source line 1486 · 2026-05-26T06:21:51.893689+00:00_

# Blog Post Information

**Author:** Horace He in collaboration with others at Thinking Machines Lab

**Title:** "Defeating Nondeterminism in LLM Inference"

**Publication Date:** September 10, 2025

The post explores why large language model inference systems produce different results across identical requests, despite using deterministic algorithms. He and his team identify batch-size dependency as the primary culprit rather than concurrency-related floating-point issues, and they present methods to achieve truly reproducible LLM inference through batch-invariant kernel implementations.

### User

_source line 1560 · 2026-05-26T06:39:17.765668+00:00_

[飞书用户 曹晓玉]: 🔍 匿名化最后扫雷：日期 + 内部编号

发现有些论文里残留了**具体实验日期**和**内部编号**——这两种东西是 reviewer 一查就能反向定位作者 / 团队的硬证据，必须彻底清掉。

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
1. 必须删 / 改：具体日期
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

任何能锁到一周内的时间点都不允许出现。包括但不限于：

❌ "Experiments were conducted on May 18, 2026..."
❌ "We collected data between 2026-04-10 and 2026-04-25..."
❌ "Models were trained on 2026/05/15..."
❌ "Snapshot from 2026-Q1..."
❌ "as of October 2025"
❌ "the recent release of GPT-5 on July 22..."

替换为相对 / 模糊表述：

✅ "Experiments were conducted in recent months."
✅ "Data was collected over a two-week period."
✅ "Models were trained for X GPU-hours."

唯一例外：引用别人论文 / 数据集发布时间（"Llama-3 (Meta, 2024)" 这种是 citation，不算）。

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
2. 必须删 / 改：内部编号
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

任何内部追溯编号都不允许出现：

❌ Experiment ID: "exp_2026_0518_a3" / "run_42_v7"
❌ WandB run ID / wandb.ai 链接
❌ 项目内部 codename（如 cost-verify-agent-rl 这种）
❌ Git commit hash
❌ Internal ticket / Jira ID
❌ Server hostname / GPU node 名（"trained on cqa1-33015"）
❌ 内部文件夹路径（"/home/USER/exp_logs/..."）
❌ Orchestration 工具名（Ralph / Director / Manager / Agent 等）

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
3. 容易漏检的位置
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

不只检查正文。下列位置最容易残留：

- Figure / Table caption
- Appendix 里的 log snippet / yaml dump / shell output

_source line 1574 · 2026-05-26T06:40:05.171081+00:00_

50	\paragraph{Null calibration.} We generate 100 Bernoulli-distributed datasets with item difficulties matched to the empirical distribution but no systematic facet effects. The observed-to-null variance ratio is 1.0008, confirming that Henderson Method~I does not introduce systematic bias when applied to binary outcomes. The null simulation also verifies that the item variance share (58.4\%) is not an artifact of the Bernoulli $p(1{-}p)$ ceiling: the null produces item variance of 0.08\%, three orders of magnitude below the observed value. Across all 100 replications, the same three components (item, model$\times$item, residual) emerge as the top three (set consistency 100\%), with Kendall's $\tau = 0.81$ between binary and continuous rankings.
51	
52	\paragraph{Reduced crossing validation.} To verify that the reduced crossing in exp-002 (BF16 only $\times$ 2 temperatures) does not introduce confounding, we extracted the corresponding subset from the fully crossed exp-001 design. The generalizability coefficient differs by only 0.0008 ($G = 0.9361$ vs.\ $0.9369$), and all non-precision variance components differ by less than 2 percentage points. The 10pp increase in item variance share (58.4\% $\to$ 68.5\%) is fully explained by the removal of precision-related components ($\sim$4.5\% total), which redistributes across remaining sources. This confirms that the exp-002 design preserves the variance structure of the full crossing.
53	
54	\section{Projection Validation Details}
55	\label{app:dstudy_cross}
56	
57	\paragraph{Internal consistency check.} Table~\ref{tab:forward_pred} reports consistency checks from four exp-001 models (DeepSeek, Llama, Mistral, Qwen3) to the full eight-model design across all five benchmarks. MC benchmarks show $|\Delta G| \leq 0.029$ ($<$3.5\% error), while FF benchmarks show larger deviations ($|\Delta G| \leq 0.065$, $\sim$7\%) due to limited model diversity in the four-model subset. _source line 1576 · 2026-05-26T06:40:05.472923+00:00_

1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Variance Sources in LLM Evaluation.}
5	\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced ANOVA-based variance decomposition for ML reproducibility, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of benchmark variance. Subsequent work isolated individual sources: \citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\% accuracy variation from numerical precision; \citet{sclar2024formatting} and BrittleBench \citep{brittlebench2026} found 50--76\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting; \citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and \citet{bui2025seeds} documented ordering and seed sensitivity. \citet{messina2026background} formalized CUDA nondeterminism from floating-point non-associativity. Each study holds other factors fixed, precluding interaction estimation. \citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor metrics without crossing factors; holistic evaluation frameworks \citep{liang2023helm, gao2024lmeval} reduce implementation variance but do not decompose it. Our work crosses seven facets simultaneously, enabling interaction estimation and cross-format comparison (Figure~\ref{fig:method_comparison}).
6	
7	\begin{figure}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_method_comparison}
10	\caption{Scope comparison with prior multi-facet variance studies. Our work uniquely combines broad facet crossing, multi-benchmark coverage, and D-study budget projections.}

_source line 1666 · 2026-05-26T07:48:45.481023+00:00_

[飞书用户 游承松 (ou_c956d29dccfbcf9d85fe1bd2e48c2b97)]: 请帮我按以下意见修改这篇论文的写作,只改措辞和表述,不改实验和数据:
1. 摘要里"dominant non-residual variance source"的说法要改。 因为 Table 5 里 item_id(37.9%)其实比 model×item(30.2%)还大,用"非残差"这个限定词会让审稿人觉得在回避 item_id。改成更直白的表述:item difficulty 是最大的方差成分,而 model×item 是出乎意料的第二大成分,关键在于它超过了所有我们操纵的 prompt、seed、precision、ordering 等设计因素。摘要、引言、结论三处都要统一成这个口径。
2. 统一"10×"和"3.1×"两个倍数。 全文在 552→178(3.1×)和 552→55(10×)之间反复横跳,容易显得标题党。请确立 3-prompt×3-seed(3.1×)为主推数字,凡是出现 10× 的地方都明确标注它对应的是 full 1296-condition 设计、属于理论上限、实践中不推荐。
3. 给贡献 C3(format-associated patterns)降调。 摘要里现在用陈述句"The variance structure is format-dependent"断言,但这是 n=5、p=0.10、CI 跨零的探索性发现,语气过强,且和正文(更克制)不一致。摘要里也要加上 exploratory、n=5 的限定,改成"we observe a format-associated pattern"这类弱化措辞。
4. 引言里"surprising discovery"改掉。 自称 surprising 有风险,改成让对比本身产生反差,比如用"contrary to the prevailing focus on prompt/seed sensitivity"这种结构。
5. §3.5 里"所有结论依赖二值评分"这一点要提为独立、加重的句子。 现在 text-exact-match(G 从 0.936 崩到 0.007)和 partial-credit(0.86→0.77)这两个重要警示是以从句一闪而过的。请把"All findings are metric-conditional"单独成句并前置强调,这是审稿人一定会问的点。
6. Limitations 里 contamination 那句要补充。 现在只有一句话。请说明污染如何具体威胁核心发现:被所有模型记住的 item 会被普遍答对,从而人为压低 model×item 交互、抬高 item 层面的一致性。
7. 引言第三段(Three lines of work...)的文献铺陈拆成更清晰的平行结构,让"前人缺口→我们填补"的逻辑更一目了然,并指向 Table 1。
修改时请保持论文整体诚实、克制的语气,不要新增任何夸大表述。

### Tool result

_source line 1668 · 2026-05-26T07:48:57.595239+00:00_

1	LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding decisions about deployment, funding, and public rankings. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) that are typically fixed to a single value and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting alone can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously, and how many test items are truly needed for reliable measurement. We address this using Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight open-weight models and five benchmarks (2.3M binary records), we find that the dominant non-residual variance source is not the prompt or the seed but the model$\times$item interaction (30--36\% in multiple-choice benchmarks, 15--17\% in free-form), meaning models disagree on which items are difficult rather than in overall ability. Multi-facet designs absorb this noise, reducing item requirements from over 1,000 to roughly 100. The variance structure is format-dependent: item factors dominate multiple-choice benchmarks but disperse in free-form tasks. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
2

1	\begin{figure*}[t]
2	\centering
3	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_overview_pipeline}
4	\caption{Overview of the G-theory evaluation pipeline. A factorial design crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks produces 2.3M binary records. G-study variance decomposition via Henderson Method~I reveals that model$\times$item interaction accounts for up to 36\% of variance. D-study projections translate variance components into design-dependent item budgets: a 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 items (3.1$\times$ reduction). Three contributions emerge: reproducibility budgets (C1), dominant model$\times$item interaction (C2), and format-associated variance patterns (C3).}
5	\label{fig:overview}
6	\end{figure*}
7	
8	\section{Introduction}
9	\label{sec:introduction}
10	
11	LLM benchmarks are the standard mechanism for comparing language models. Evaluation results determine which models get deployed in production, which attract continued funding, and which top public leaderboards. A typical evaluation runs each model once under a fixed configuration (one prompt template, one random seed, one precision format) and treats the resulting score as a stable measure of model capability. This practice implicitly assumes that these design choices are inconsequential: that a different prompt wording, a different random seed, or a different answer ordering would yield essentially the same result.
12	
13	A growing body of evidence shows otherwise. Prompt reformatting (rewording instructions without altering their meaning) can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points \citep{sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026}. Reordering answer options can reverse model rankings \citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering}. Numerical precision \citep{yuan2025layercast} and random seeds \citep{dodge2020finetuning, bui2025seeds} each contribute additional instability. However, each study examines one source in isolation, holding all others fixed. This pieceme

74	\subsection{Binary Data Assumptions}
75	\label{sec:binary}
76	
77	Standard G-theory assumes continuous data \cite{shavelson1991generalizability}; our binary scores violate this. Henderson Method~I produces unbiased estimates for balanced designs regardless of distribution \cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}, and restricted maximum likelihood (REML) differs by $<$0.5pp. A probit simulation, generalized linear mixed model (GLMM) complement ($\rho = 1.0$), and accuracy-range analysis confirm robustness (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}).
78	
79	All findings are conditioned on binary correctness scoring; the text-exact-match comparison in \S\ref{sec:single_model} ($G = 0.007$ vs.\ $0.936$) illustrates how scoring can reshape variance structure. Partial-credit analysis on GSM8K confirms $G_\text{item}$ drops from 0.86 to 0.77 (mean $\Delta G = -0.135$; Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}), suggesting limited transferability to finer-grained rubrics.
80	
81	\subsection{Model Facet Specification}

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	The dominant non-residual variance source in LLM evaluation is the model$\times$item interaction (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in FF), revealing that models disagree on which items are difficult rather than differing uniformly in overall ability. Multi-facet evaluation designs absorb this interaction: a 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under single-condition, with cross-benchmark budgets of 93--128 items. The variance structure is format-dependent: MC benchmarks concentrate 68--72\% in item-related components while FF benchmarks disperse to 31--41\%, motivating per-format budget calibration. We recommend three practices: adopt multi-facet designs for published results, report item-level accuracy to expose model$\times$item interaction, and calibrate item budgets per benchmark format.
5

1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	Our design covers eight 7--9B instruction-tuned models; preliminary 72B validation confirms consistent variance structure, but broader scale and temporal coverage is needed. The benchmark sample ($n = 5$) limits statistical power for format-associated observations ($p = 0.10$). All D-study budgets assume binary scoring; partial-credit analysis on GSM8K shows $G$ drops from 0.86 to 0.77, suggesting limited transferability to finer-grained rubrics. Benchmark contamination in pretraining data may inflate item accuracy and compress variance; our design does not control for this source.
4

1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	All experiments use open-weight 7--9B parameter models evaluated with vLLM~0.9.2 on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (96GB) with binary correctness scoring.
4	
5	\subsection{Single-Model Baseline}
6	\label{sec:single_model}
7	
8	\begin{figure}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_single_model_variance}
11	\caption{Single-model variance decomposition (Llama-3.1-8B, MMLU). Item difficulty dominates (58.4\%); all main facet effects are $<$1\%. Item-conditional interactions total 17.2\%.}
12	\label{fig:single_model_var}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	\paragraph{Design.} The G-study crosses six facets: 200 items $\times$ 3 numerical precisions (BF16, FP16, FP32) $\times$ 6 prompt templates $\times$ 6 random seeds $\times$ 3 temperature levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) $\times$ 4 answer orderings, yielding 259,200 binary observations. Variance components are estimated via Henderson Method~I with bootstrap confidence intervals (1,000 resamples).
16	
17	\paragraph{Results.} Item difficulty dominates at 58.4\%, with residual at 23.1\% (Figure~\ref{fig:single_model_var}). All main facet effects are negligible ($<$1\% each); the interesting structure is in item-conditional interactions (17.2\% total), where prompt$\times$item (5.5\%) is largest. The generalizability coefficient $G_\text{item} = 0.936$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:gcoeff}); D-study projection (Eq.~\ref{eq:dstudy}) gives $G \geq 0.80$ with 55 items.
18	
19	For text-exact-match scoring, temperature dominates at 79.7\% and $G_\text{item}$ collapses to 0.007, motivating our exclusive use of binary correctness.
20	
21	\subsection{Cross-Model Variance}
22	
23	Extending to eight models (BF16 subset, 460,800 records), Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} shows that item difficulty drops from 58.4\% to 37.9\%, while model$\times$item interaction emerges at 30.2\% (up to 36\% in MC benchmarks, 15--17\% in FF; \S\ref{sec:format_conditional}). The model main effect is 3--5\% (MC) and 9--11\% (FF)\footnote{Ranges r

```json
    "new_string": "contrary to the prevailing focus on prompt and seed sensitivity, item difficulty is the largest variance component (34--38\\% in MC), and the model$\\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\\% in MC, 15--17\\% in FF), exceeding every manipulated design factor by an order of magnitude. Models disagree primarily on \\emph{which specific questions} are difficult, rather than differing uniformly in overall ability. This finding has an immediate practical consequence: evaluation designs that average over multiple prompts and seeds absorb this interaction, achieving reliable measurement with a recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed protocol that reduces items by 3.1$\\times$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}). In an exploratory comparison ($n = 5$ benchmarks), item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice benchmarks but disperses in free-form tasks, suggesting that reproducibility standards may need per-format calibration."
  }
```
```json
}
```

```json
  "old_string": "All findings are conditioned on binary correctness scoring; the text-exact-match comparison in \\S\\ref{sec:single_model} ($G = 0.007$ vs.\\ $0.936$) illustrates how scoring can reshape variance structure. Partial-credit analysis on GSM8K confirms $G_\\text{item}$ drops from 0.86 to 0.77 (mean $\\Delta G = -0.135$; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}), suggesting limited transferability to finer-grained rubrics.",
  "new_string": "All findings in this paper are conditional on binary correctness scoring. Changing the scoring metric can fundamentally reshape variance structure: text-exact-match scoring collapses $G_\\text{item}$ from 0.936 to 0.007 (\\S\\ref{sec:single_model}), and partial-credit scoring on GSM8K reduces $G_\\text{item}$ from 0.86 to 0.77 (mean $\\Delta G = -0.135$; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}). Readers should not assume that the variance decomposition or D-study budgets reported here transfer to non-binary rubrics."
}
```

```json
  "old_string": "The dominant non-residual variance source in LLM evaluation is the model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\% in MC, 15--17\\% in FF), revealing that models disagree on which items are difficult rather than differing uniformly in overall ability. Multi-facet evaluation designs absorb this interaction: a 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under single-condition, with cross-benchmark budgets of 93--128 items. The variance structure is format-dependent: MC benchmarks concentrate 68--72\\% in item-related components while FF benchmarks disperse to 31--41\\%, motivating per-format budget calibration. We recommend three practices: adopt multi-facet designs for published results, report item-level accuracy to expose model$\\times$item interaction, and calibrate item budgets per benchmark format.",
  "new_string": "Item difficulty is the largest variance component in LLM evaluation (34--38\\% in MC), and the model$\\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\\% in MC, 15--17\\% in FF), exceeding all manipulated design factors by an order of magnitude. This reveals that models disagree on which items are difficult rather than differing uniformly in overall ability. Multi-facet evaluation designs absorb this interaction: a recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \\geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under single-condition protocols (3.1$\\times$ reduction), with cross-benchmark budgets of 93--128 items. An exploratory format comparison ($n = 5$ benchmarks) suggests MC benchmarks concentrate 68--72\\% in item-related components while FF benchmarks disperse to 31--41\\%, motivating per-format budget calibration pending broader confirmation. We recommend three practices: adopt multi-facet designs for published results, report item-level accuracy to expose model$\\times$item interaction, and calibrate item budgets per benchmark format."
}
```

```json
  "old_string": "Benchmark contamination in pretraining data may inflate item accuracy and compress variance; our design does not control for this source.",
  "new_string": "Benchmark contamination in pretraining data may systematically bias our variance decomposition: items memorized by all models would be universally answered correctly, artificially deflating model$\\times$item interaction (by reducing inter-model disagreement on those items) and inflating item-level consistency, making D-study budgets optimistic. Our design does not control for contamination; future work should incorporate contamination-aware item sampling."
}
```

_source line 1712 · 2026-05-26T07:55:08.586328+00:00_

1	LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding deployment, funding, and ranking decisions. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) typically fixed to single values and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting alone can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously, and how many items suffice for reliable measurement. We address this with Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight open-weight models and five benchmarks (2.3M binary records), we find that item difficulty is the largest variance component (34--38\% in multiple-choice), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in free-form), exceeding every manipulated factor (prompt, seed, precision, ordering) by an order of magnitude---models disagree on which items are difficult, not in overall ability. A 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol absorbs this interaction, reducing items from 552 to 178 ($G \geq 0.80$). We observe a format-associated pattern ($n = 5$, exploratory): item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68--72\%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31--41\%). We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
2

1	LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding deployment, funding, and ranking decisions. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) typically fixed to single values and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting can shift accuracy by 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously and how many items suffice. We address this with Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks (2.3M records), we find that item difficulty is the largest variance component (34--38\% in multiple-choice), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in free-form), exceeding every manipulated factor (prompt, seed, precision, ordering) by an order of magnitude---models disagree on which items are difficult, not in overall ability. A 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol absorbs this interaction, reducing items from 552 to 178 ($G \geq 0.80$). We observe a format-associated pattern ($n = 5$, exploratory): item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68--72\%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31--41\%). ### Tool result

_source line 1742 · 2026-05-26T08:13:51.556501+00:00_

1	LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding deployment, funding, and ranking decisions. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) typically fixed to single values and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting can shift accuracy by 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously and how many items suffice. We address this with Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks (2.3M records), we find that item difficulty is the largest variance component (34--38\% in multiple-choice), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in free-form), exceeding every manipulated factor by an order of magnitude---models disagree on which items are difficult, not in overall ability. A 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol absorbs this interaction, reducing items from 552 to 178 ($G \geq 0.80$). We observe a format-associated pattern ($n = 5$, exploratory): item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68--72\%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31--41\%). We release an open-source G-theory toolkit\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
2

1	\begin{figure*}[t]
2	\centering
3	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_overview_pipeline}
4	\caption{Overview of the G-theory evaluation pipeline. A factorial design crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks produces 2.3M binary records. G-study variance decomposition via Henderson Method~I reveals that item difficulty (34--38\%) and model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%) together dominate in MC benchmarks. D-study projections translate variance components into design-dependent item budgets: a 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 items (3.1$\times$ reduction). Three contributions emerge: reproducibility budgets (C1), dominant model$\times$item interaction (C2), and format-associated variance patterns (C3).}
5	\label{fig:overview}
6	\end{figure*}

### Tool result

_source line 1746 · 2026-05-26T08:13:52.962918+00:00_

19	Our contributions:
20	\begin{enumerate}
21	    \item \textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} A recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under single-condition protocols (3.1$\times$ reduction; up to 10$\times$ under the full 1,296-condition design, a theoretical upper bound impractical for routine use). Cross-benchmark budgets range from 93--128 items (multi-facet) versus 1,078--2,768 (single-condition). These budgets complement IRT item selection (Table~\ref{tab:dstudy}).
22	
23	    \item \textbf{Dominant item-level variance.} Item difficulty is the largest single component in MC benchmarks (34--38\%), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in FF), exceeding every manipulated factor (prompt, seed, precision, ordering) and the model main effect by 7--11$\times$. This reveals item-dependent rather than global ability differences, stable under leave-one-model-out analysis and confirmed by inter-model agreement (Cohen's $\kappa = 0.33$--$0.46$).
24	
25	    \item \textbf{Format-associated variance patterns (exploratory).} Item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice benchmarks (68--72\%) but disperses in free-form benchmarks (31--41\%), driven by item difficulty heterogeneity ($\rho = 0.82$). This observation ($n = 5$, $p = 0.10$) warrants broader confirmation.
26	\end{enumerate}
27	We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.
28	
29	\begin{figure}[t]
30	\centering
31	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig0_teaser_dstudy}
32	\caption{D-study projections for MMLU (single-model baseline). A recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design reduces the budget from 552 to 178 items for $G \geq 0.80$; the full multi-facet design reduces further to 55 items.}
33	\label{fig:teaser}
34	\end{figure}

21	\subsection{Cross-Model Variance}
22	
23	Extending to eight models (BF16 subset, 460,800 records), Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} shows that item difficulty drops from 58.4\% to 37.9\%, while model$\times$item interaction emerges at 30.2\% (up to 36\% in MC benchmarks, 15--17\% in FF; \S\ref{sec:format_conditional}). The model main effect is 3--5\% (MC) and 9--11\% (FF)\footnote{Ranges rounded to nearest integer; exact values are 2.8--5.4\% (MC) and 8.5--10.7\% (FF) per Table~\ref{tab:cross_benchmark}.}, suggesting that models differ primarily in \emph{which items} they find difficult rather than in overall ability level. Figure~\ref{fig:item_heatmap} visualizes this pattern across models and benchmarks. Leave-one-model-out (LOMO) analysis (Table~\ref{tab:lomo_full}) confirms this structure is stable: no single model removal changes $G$ by more than 0.030 across all five benchmarks. Inter-model agreement independently quantifies this pattern: pairwise Cohen's $\kappa$ ranges from 0.326 (MATH) to 0.461 (MMLU), indicating only fair-to-moderate agreement on individual items despite similar aggregate accuracy, consistent with models differing in \emph{which items} they solve rather than in overall ability (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}).
24	
25	\begin{table}[t]

_source line 1798 · 2026-05-26T08:19:36.850751+00:00_

[飞书用户 曹晓玉]: 🔍 论文自检：前向引用问题（先用后定义）

发现有些论文里出现"先用后定义"的情况——某个概念 / 符号 / 模块 / 缩写在 Section X 里直接用了，但它的定义放在 Section X+N 才出现。这是 AI 写作的典型问题，reviewer 读到这里会觉得"不知所云、组织混乱"。

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
检查范围
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

每个东西第一次出现的位置，必须**已经**完成定义。包括：

□ 缩写 / 简写（如 RLHF / DPO / IRT，第一次出现要写全称 + 缩写）
□ 自定义概念 / 术语（你 paper 自己造的词，如 "behavioral death"、"sycophancy score"）
□ 数学符号 / 公式中的变量（每个变量在第一次出现的方程里就要解释）
□ 自定义方法 / 模块组件（"the consistency gate"、"the alignment head"）
□ 评测指标（自定义的 metric，必须在 main result 表出现之前定义清楚）
□ 数据集 / setting 的内部命名（"the strict split"、"the holdout pool"）

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
常见违规模式
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

❌ Abstract / Intro 里用了一个自造术语，Method 才定义
❌ Intro 提到 "we use SYCONScore"，到 §4 才看到这个 metric 的定义
❌ Method §2 用了变量 α，到 §2.3 才写 "where α denotes..."
❌ Method 提到 "module M"，结构图 + 解释在 Appendix 才有
❌ Table 1 列了 "Score@K"，K 在 §5 才说取什么值

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
修复策略
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

每个违规位置二选一：

A) **把定义提前**到第一次使用之前。最干净。
B) 在第一次使用的位置**加一个简短的 inline 解释**（一句话 / 括号说明）+ "(formally defined in §X)" 引用前向。容忍度更高的妥协方案。

注意：A 优先。只有 A 会改动太多结构时，才退而求其次用 B。

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
汇报内容
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

(a) 找到多少处先用后定义？分布在哪些章节？
(b) 每一处用 A 还是 B 修的？
(c) 修完后 PDF 编译通过

_source line 1800 · 2026-05-26T08:19:49.167192+00:00_

1	LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding deployment, funding, and ranking decisions. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) typically fixed to single values and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting can shift accuracy by 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously and how many items suffice. We address this with Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks (2.3M records), we find that item difficulty is the largest variance component (34--38\% in multiple-choice), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in free-form), exceeding every manipulated design factor by an order of magnitude---models disagree on which items are difficult, not in overall ability. A 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol absorbs this interaction, reducing items from 552 to 178 ($G \geq 0.80$). We observe a format-associated pattern ($n = 5$, exploratory): item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68--72\%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31--41\%). We release an open-source G-theory toolkit\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
2

1	\begin{figure*}[t]
2	\centering
3	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_overview_pipeline}
4	\caption{Overview of the G-theory evaluation pipeline. A factorial design crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks produces 2.3M binary records. G-study variance decomposition via Henderson Method~I reveals that item difficulty (34--38\%) and model$\times$item interaction (30--36\%) together dominate in MC benchmarks. D-study projections translate variance components into design-dependent item budgets: a 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 items (3.1$\times$ reduction). Three contributions emerge: reproducibility budgets (C1), dominant item-level variance (C2), and format-associated variance patterns (C3).}
5	\label{fig:overview}
6	\end{figure*}

8	\section{Introduction}
9	\label{sec:introduction}
10	
11	LLM benchmarks are the standard mechanism for comparing language models. Evaluation results determine which models get deployed in production, which attract continued funding, and which top public leaderboards. A typical evaluation runs each model once under a fixed configuration (one prompt template, one random seed, one precision format) and treats the resulting score as a stable measure of model capability. This practice implicitly assumes that these design choices are inconsequential: that a different prompt wording, a different random seed, or a different answer ordering would yield essentially the same result.
12	
13	A growing body of evidence shows otherwise. Prompt reformatting (rewording instructions without altering their meaning) can shift accuracy by up to 76 percentage points \citep{sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026}. Reordering answer options can reverse model rankings \citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering}. Numerical precision \citep{yuan2025layercast} and random seeds \citep{dodge2020finetuning, bui2025seeds} each contribute additional instability. 1	\section{Method and Experimental Design}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Generalizability Theory}
5	\label{sec:gtheory}
6	
7	Generalizability theory (G-theory) treats each measurement as one observation from a universe of admissible conditions defined by the crossed facets of an evaluation design \cite{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability}. The \emph{object of measurement} is the entity whose consistency is of interest. In our setting, test items ($p$) serve as the object of measurement, and the goal is to determine how reliably item-level scores generalize across the methodological conditions under which they are collected.
8	
9	A \emph{G-study} decomposes total observed variance into components $\sigma^2_x$ for each facet $x$ and their interactions. Each component quantifies how much variability that source contributes to the overall measurement. Large interaction components (e.g., $\sigma^2_{pM}$ for item-by-model) indicate that facets do not affect items uniformly but instead create differential difficulty profiles. We estimate variance components via Henderson Method~I, an unbiased ANOVA-based estimator for balanced designs \cite[Ch.~3]{brennan2001generalizability}, with 1,000-resample bootstrap confidence intervals \cite{efron1993bootstrap} (item-level resampling with replacement, preserving the fully-crossed structure across all other facets) \cite{li2023bootstrap}.
10	
11	A \emph{D-study} projects the generalizability coefficient under hypothetical designs with different numbers of conditions per facet:
12	\begin{equation}
13	G_\text{item} = \frac{\sigma^2_p}{\sigma^2_p + \sigma^2_\text{rel}}
14	\label{eq:gcoeff}
15	\end{equation}
16	where $\sigma^2_\text{rel} = \sum_i \sigma^2_{p \times f_i} / n_{f_i} + \sigma^2_\text{residual} / \prod_i n_{f_i}$ aggregates all item-by-facet interaction variances, each divided by the number of conditions for the corresponding facets \cite[Ch.~4]{brennan2001generalizability}. A D-study projects 
…[truncated 8376 chars]

1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	All experiments use open-weight 7--9B parameter models evaluated with vLLM~0.9.2 on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (96GB) with binary correctness scoring.
4	
5	\subsection{Single-Model Baseline}
6	\label{sec:single_model}
7	
8	\begin{figure}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_single_model_variance}
11	\caption{Single-model variance decomposition (Llama-3.1-8B, MMLU). Item difficulty dominates (58.4\%); all main facet effects are $<$1\%. Item-conditional interactions total 17.2\%.}
12	\label{fig:single_model_var}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	\paragraph{Design.} The G-study crosses six facets: 200 items $\times$ 3 numerical precisions (BF16, FP16, FP32) $\times$ 6 prompt templates $\times$ 6 random seeds $\times$ 3 temperature levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) $\times$ 4 answer orderings, yielding 259,200 binary observations. Variance components are estimated via Henderson Method~I with bootstrap confidence intervals (1,000 resamples).
16	
17	\paragraph{Results.} Item difficulty dominates at 58.4\%, with residual at 23.1\% (Figure~\ref{fig:single_model_var}). All main facet effects are negligible ($<$1\% each); the interesting structure is in item-conditional interactions (17.2\% total), where prompt$\times$item (5.5\%) is largest. The generalizability coefficient $G_\text{item} = 0.936$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:gcoeff}); D-study projection (Eq.~\ref{eq:dstudy}) gives $G \geq 0.80$ with 55 items.
18	
19	For text-exact-match scoring, temperature dominates at 79.7\% and $G_\text{item}$ collapses to 0.007, motivating our exclusive use of binary correctness.
20

_source line 1810 · 2026-05-26T08:19:56.147711+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Item difficulty is the largest variance component in LLM evaluation (34--38\% in MC), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in FF), exceeding all manipulated design factors by an order of magnitude. This reveals that models disagree on which items are difficult rather than differing uniformly in overall ability. Multi-facet evaluation designs absorb this interaction: a recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under single-condition protocols (3.1$\times$ reduction), with cross-benchmark budgets of 93--128 items. An exploratory format comparison ($n = 5$ benchmarks) suggests MC benchmarks concentrate 68--72\% in item-related components while FF benchmarks disperse to 31--41\%, motivating per-format budget calibration pending broader confirmation. We recommend three practices: adopt multi-facet designs for published results, report item-level accuracy to expose model$\times$item interaction, and calibrate item budgets per benchmark format.
5

1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Additional Robustness Analyses}
4	\label{app:robustness}
5	
6	We report nine robustness analyses that verify the stability of the variance decomposition under alternative analytical choices.
7	
8	\paragraph{GLMM complement.} A generalized linear mixed model (GLMM) with marginal logit link produces variance component rankings that perfectly preserve all seven grouped component orderings ($\rho = 1.0$) relative to Henderson Method~I \cite{jiang2018bayesian, tuerlinckx2006glmm}. This confirms that the linear variance decomposition is robust to the binary outcome distribution.
9	
10	\paragraph{Accuracy-range stability.} The \texttt{item\_id} variance share remains stable across subsets with mean accuracy from 0.62 to 0.72 ($r = -0.048$). This rules out distortion from binary floor or ceiling effects at observed accuracy levels.
11	
12	\paragraph{Estimator robustness.} We compare Henderson Method~I with restricted maximum likelihood (REML-EM, 500 iterations, practical convergence $\Delta < 0.003$ percentage points). All variance components differ by $<$0.5 percentage points between estimators. The largest discrepancy occurs on the residual component (0.47pp); all substantive components (item, model$\times$item, model) differ by $<$0.3pp. Henderson Method~I is preferred for our balanced designs because it produces unbiased estimates without distributional assumptions \cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}.
13	
14	\paragraph{Probit simulation.} A probit simulation matched to our 1,296-condition design confirms that Henderson Method~I recovers variance component rankings from dichotomized data (Spearman $\rho = 0.732$ between binary and continuous rankings, excluding residual; \texttt{item\_id} dominant in all 100 replications; top-5 ordering correct in 79\% of replications). Bias analysis by difficulty stratum shows $|\text{bias}| = 0.28$--$0.37$ for items with $p \in [0.5, 0.8]$, where the Bernoulli variance ceiling $p(1{-}p)$ is largest. Thi
…[truncated 21343 chars]

_source line 1818 · 2026-05-26T08:20:06.783049+00:00_

1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Variance Sources in LLM Evaluation.}
5	\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced ANOVA-based variance decomposition for ML reproducibility, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of benchmark variance. Subsequent work isolated individual sources: \citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\% accuracy variation from numerical precision; \citet{sclar2024formatting} and BrittleBench \citep{brittlebench2026} found 50--76\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting; \citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and \citet{bui2025seeds} documented ordering and seed sensitivity. \citet{messina2026background} formalized CUDA nondeterminism from floating-point non-associativity. Each study holds other factors fixed, precluding interaction estimation. \citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor metrics without crossing factors; holistic evaluation frameworks \citep{liang2023helm, gao2024lmeval} reduce implementation variance but do not decompose it. Our work crosses seven facets simultaneously, enabling interaction estimation and cross-format comparison (Figure~\ref{fig:method_comparison}).
6	
7	\begin{figure}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_method_comparison}
10	\caption{Scope comparison with prior multi-facet variance studies. Our work uniquely combines broad facet crossing, multi-benchmark coverage, and D-study budget projections.}
11	\label{fig:method_comparison}
12	\end{figure}
13	
14	\paragraph{IRT and Efficient Benchmarking.}
15	IRT models item difficulty and discrimination to optimize benchmark efficiency \citep{lalor2019irt, rodriguez2021leaderboards}. \citet{polo2024tinybenchmarks} and \citet{zhou2026irt} developed IRT-based subsampling for efficient evaluation; \citet{perlitz2023efficient} and \citet{kipnis2025metabench} explored related compression strategies. These approaches optimize item selection but do not model variance from prompt, seed, or ordering 
…[truncated 2040 chars]

_source line 1826 · 2026-05-26T08:22:33.353916+00:00_

19	Our contributions:
20	\begin{enumerate}
21	    \item \textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} A recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under single-condition protocols (3.1$\times$ reduction; up to 10$\times$ under the full 1,296-condition design, a theoretical upper bound impractical for routine use). Cross-benchmark budgets range from 93--128 items (multi-facet) versus 1,078--2,768 (single-condition). These budgets complement IRT item selection (Table~\ref{tab:dstudy}).
22	
23	    \item \textbf{Dominant item-level variance.} Item difficulty is the largest single component in MC benchmarks (34--38\%), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in FF), exceeding the model main effect by 7--11$\times$ and every manipulated design factor (prompt, seed, precision, ordering) by over an order of magnitude. This reveals item-dependent rather than global ability differences, stable under leave-one-model-out analysis and confirmed by inter-model agreement (Cohen's $\kappa = 0.33$--$0.46$).
24	
25	    \item \textbf{Format-associated variance patterns (exploratory).} Item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice benchmarks (68--72\%) but disperses in free-form benchmarks (31--41\%), driven by item difficulty heterogeneity ($\rho = 0.82$). This observation ($n = 5$, $p = 0.10$) warrants broader confirmation.
26	\end{enumerate}
27	We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.
28

77:Standard G-theory assumes continuous data \cite{shavelson1991generalizability}; our binary scores violate this. Henderson Method~I produces unbiased estimates for balanced designs regardless of distribution \cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}, and restricted maximum likelihood (REML) differs by $<$0.5pp. A probit simulation, generalized linear mixed model (GLMM) complement ($\rho = 1.0$), and accuracy-range analysis confirm robustness (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}).

# Qwen3 Technical Report

**Title:** "Qwen3 Technical Report"

**Yes, this is about Qwen3** — the latest iteration of the Qwen model family from Alibaba.

**First 5 Authors:**
1. An Yang
2. Anfeng Li
3. Baosong Yang
4. Beichen Zhang
5. Binyuan Hui

The paper was submitted to arXiv on May 14, 2025, and describes a series of large language models ranging from 0.6 to 235 billion parameters, featuring both dense and mixture-of-experts architectures with integrated thinking and non-thinking modes.

# Yi Paper Details

**Exact Title:** "Yi: Open Foundation Models by 01.AI"

**First 5 Authors:**
1. Alex Young
2. Bei Chen
3. Chao Li
4. Chengen Huang
5. Ge Zhang

**About Yi Models:** Yes, this research describes the Yi model family created by 01.AI. The paper introduces a series of language and multimodal models available in 6B and 34B sizes, along with extended versions featuring expanded context windows, enhanced depth, and vision-language capabilities. The authors emphasize that their models' strong performance stems primarily from meticulous data engineering, including 3.1 trillion tokens of curated English and Chinese training materials.

1	"""vLLM offline inference runner for the G-theory variance study.
2	
3	Loads a model once via ``vllm.LLM`` and supports batched generation
4	with explicit seed control at three levels:
5	  1. ``LLM(seed=...)``           — engine-level RNG
6	  2. ``torch.cuda.manual_seed``  — CUDA kernel RNG
7	  3. ``SamplingParams(seed=...)`` — per-request sampling RNG
8	
9	CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES is set from ``gpu_id`` *before* any CUDA call.
10	
11	Input:  model name + generation parameters.
12	Output: list of ``InferenceResult`` dataclass instances (JSON-serialisable).
13	"""
14	
15	import argparse
16	import json
17	import os
18	import time
19	from dataclasses import asdict, dataclass
20	from typing import Optional

22	import torch

25

27	class InferenceResult:
28	    prompt: str
29	    generated_text: str
30	    finish_reason: str
31	    model: str
32	    dtype: str
33	    temperature: float
34	    top_p: Optional[float]
35	    seed: int
36	    gpu_id: int
37	    latency_ms: float
38	    answer_logprob: Optional[float] = None
39	    top_logprobs: Optional[dict] = None
40	
41	
42	class VLLMRunner:
43	    """Manages a single vLLM model instance for batched inference.
44	
45	    Create one runner per (model, dtype, gpu_id) combination to avoid
46	    model reloads.  Different temperatures / seeds reuse the same runner.
47	    """

1	"""Execute the full G-theory experiment from a design matrix.
2	
3	Reads the factorial design CSV, groups conditions by (model, precision) to
4	minimise model reloads, then for every condition × item constructs a prompt
5	and runs vLLM inference.  Results are scored via exact-match extraction of
6	A/B/C/D from generated text and written to a JSONL file.
7	
8	Input:

1	\section{Method and Experimental Design}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Generalizability Theory}
5	\label{sec:gtheory}
6	
7	Generalizability theory (G-theory) treats each measurement as one observation from a universe of admissible conditions defined by the crossed facets of an evaluation design \cite{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability}. The \emph{object of measurement} is the entity whose consistency is of interest. In our setting, test items ($p$) serve as the object of measurement, and the goal is to determine how reliably item-level scores generalize across the methodological conditions under which they are collected.
8	
9	A \emph{G-study} decomposes total observed variance into components $\sigma^2_x$ for each facet $x$ and their interactions. Each component quantifies how much variability that source contributes to the overall measurement. Large interaction components (e.g., $\sigma^2_{pM}$ for item-by-model) indicate that facets do not affect items uniformly but instead create differential difficulty profiles. We estimate variance components via Henderson Method~I, an unbiased ANOVA-based estimator for balanced designs \cite[Ch.~3]{brennan2001generalizability}, with 1,000-resample bootstrap confidence intervals \cite{efron1993bootstrap} (item-level resampling with replacement, preserving the fully-crossed structure across all other facets) \cite{li2023bootstrap}.
10	
11	A \emph{D-study} projects the generalizability coefficient under hypothetical designs with different numbers of conditions per facet:
12	\begin{equation}
13	G_\text{item} = \frac{\sigma^2_p}{\sigma^2_p + \sigma^2_\text{rel}}
14	\label{eq:gcoeff}
15	\end{equation}
16	where $\sigma^2_\text{rel} = \sum_i \sigma^2_{p \times f_i} / n_{f_i} + \sigma^2_\text{residual} / \prod_i n_{f_i}$ aggregates all item-by-facet interaction variances, each divided by the number of conditions for the corresponding facets \cite[Ch.~4]{brennan2001generalizability}. A D-study projects

72:All evaluations use generation-based scoring (free-text response with answer extraction) rather than log-likelihood ranking, following \citet{hua2025flaw}. Binary correctness (1/0) is the primary metric; text-exact-match is secondary. Inference uses vLLM~0.9.2 without batch-invariant kernels, preserving CUDA-level nondeterminism as a measurable variance source \cite{messina2026background, thinkingmachines2025batch}. Few-shot protocols: 5-shot MMLU/ARC, 4-shot MATH, 8-shot GSM8K, 10-shot HellaSwag.

1	# Idea Brief: Where Does the Variance Go? Decomposing Infrastructure and Methodological Variance in LLM Evaluation via Generalizability Theory
2	
3	
4	- **Idea ID**: `idea_031`
5	- **Created**: 2026-05-20
6	- **Approved**: 2026-05-20
7	- **Source**: foreign:be-idea-gen-0515-4
8	- **Level**: solid
9	- **Target Venues**: AAAI 2027 (abstract Jul 21, paper Jul 28 2026 — primary target with 10 weeks runway; requires overlapping experiment execution with writing), ACL 2027 via ARR (October 15, 2026 cycle — comfortable 21-week runway; preferred if AAAI timeline proves too tight), TACL (rolling monthly deadline — no conference deadline pressure, allows most thorough execution), NeurIPS 2027 (abstract ~May 2027 — very comfortable fallback if earlier deadlines are missed)
10	
11	## Seed Topic
12	
13	web_research
14	
15	## Core Problem
16	
17	When LLM evaluation results vary across reproductions, practitioners cannot determine whether the culprit is prompt phrasing, random seed, GPU hardware, numerical precision, temperature, or few-shot ordering—let alone how these factors interact—because every prior study isolates at most two sources. Mesting (2026) recently decomposed methodological sources (prompt variant, temperature, judge model) via Generalizability Theory but treated infrastructure as fixed background; no existing work crosses infrastructure factors (hardware type, numerical precision) with methodological factors (prompt, temperature, seed, data ordering) in a unified variance decomposition that produces actionable reproducibility budgets.
18	
19	## Opportunity / Why Now
20	
21	Multiple 2025–2026 papers each quantify individual variance sources in isolation: Brittlebench (2026) attributes up to 50% of performance variance to prompt perturbations and shows single perturbations alter model rankings in 63% of cases; Bui et al. (2025) document extreme seed sensitivity (RTES variance = 18.22) in fine-tuning; Yuan et al. (2025) demonstrate up to 9% accuracy variation and 
…[truncated 12688 chars]

1	#!/bin/bash
2	# Cross-model G-theory analysis pipeline (bf16 + fp32 only)
3	# Usage: bash scripts/run_cross_model_analysis.sh
4	
5	set -euo pipefail
6	
7	cd /root/nlp_variance_gtheory
8	source /root/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh && conda activate base
9	export PYTHONPATH=/root/nlp_variance_gtheory:$PYTHONPATH
10	
11	mkdir -p results/analysis
12	
13	# Step 1: Merge data (cross-model: bf16+fp32 only)

15	python -m src.analysis.merge_cross_model \
16	  --input-glob "results/exp001_*/shard_*.jsonl" \
17	  --output results/analysis/cross_model_bf16_fp32.csv \
18	  --cross-model
19	
20	# Step 2: 7-facet G-study (model as random facet)
21	echo "=== Step 2: G-study variance decomposition ==="
22	python -m src.analysis.model_facet_analysis \
23	  --input results/analysis/cross_model_bf16_fp32.csv \
24	  --output results/analysis/cross_model_gstudy.json
25	
26	# Step 3: Bootstrap CIs (200 resamples for preliminary)

29	  --input results/analysis/cross_model_bf16_fp32.csv \
30	  --n-boot 200 \
31	  --output results/analysis/cross_model_bootstrap.json
32	
33	echo "=== Done ==="

# Paper Information

**Exact Title:** "DeepSeek LLM: Scaling Open-Source Language Models with Longtermism"

**First 3 Authors:**
1. Xiao Bi
2. Deli Chen
3. Guanting Chen

**Is this about DeepSeek LLM?** Yes. This arXiv paper (2401.02954) presents DeepSeek LLM, describing how the research team developed open-source language models in 7B and 67B configurations. According to the abstract, they created "DeepSeek Chat models" through supervised fine-tuning and preference optimization, with the 67B variant reportedly outperforming comparable models on various benchmarks.

The largest model features 405 billion parameters and supports context windows up to 128K tokens.

175	\section{Model and Prompt Details}
176	\label{app:models}
177	
178	\paragraph{Models.} See Table~\ref{tab:model_ids} (main text) for the eight models. Full HuggingFace identifiers: \texttt{meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct}, \texttt{google/gemma-2-9b-it}, \texttt{mistralai/Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3}, \texttt{Qwen/Qwen3-8B}, \texttt{01-ai/Yi-1.5-9B-Chat}, \texttt{allenai/OLMo-2-1124-7B-Instruct}, \texttt{internlm/internlm2\_5-7b-chat}, \texttt{deepseek-ai/deepseek-llm-7b-chat}.
179	
180	\paragraph{Prompt templates.} Our six prompt templates follow a surface perturbation design inspired by BrittleBench \cite{brittlebench2026}. For multiple-choice benchmarks, templates vary along three dimensions: option layout (lettered vs.\ numbered), instruction wording (direct vs.\ contextual), and answer prompt cue. For free-form benchmarks, templates vary chain-of-thought elicitation strategy. All six templates are semantically equivalent: they request the same task with different surface forms, to isolate formatting effects from content effects. ARC-Challenge and HellaSwag use the same six surface patterns as MMLU with domain-appropriate system instructions; MATH mirrors the GSM8K pattern with \texttt{\textbackslash boxed\{\}} answer formatting.
181	
182	\smallskip\noindent\textit{MMLU templates (representative MC format):}
183	\begin{enumerate}\setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}\setlength{\parsep}{0pt}\setlength{\topsep}{0pt}
184	\item \texttt{The following are multiple choice questions (with answers) about \{subject\}.\textbackslash n\textbackslash n\{question\}\textbackslash nA. ... D. ...\textbackslash nAnswer:}
185	\item \texttt{Answer the following \{subject\} questions by selecting the correct option.\textbackslash nQuestion: \{question\}\textbackslash n(A) ... (B) ... (C) ... (D) ...\textbackslash nCorrect answer:}
186	\item \texttt{Below are \{subject\} multiple-choice problems. Pick the right letter.\textbackslash nQ: \{question\}\textbackslash n~~A) ... B) ... C) ... D) ...\t

### User

_source line 1990 · 2026-05-26T10:45:24.928370+00:00_

[飞书用户 曹晓玉]: 🔍 论文自检：所有引用的逐项正确性检查（交叉验证）

请对论文里**每一条引用**做严格核对。AI 写作最致命的问题之一就是 hallucinate 引用——作者错、年份错、venue 错、标题对不上，甚至整篇论文压根不存在。

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
检查范围
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

对 .bib 里的每一条 entry，以及正文里每一个 \cite{} 出现的位置：

□ 作者名拼写（中间名 / 缩写格式 / Unicode 字符）
□ 年份（很多 AI 把 NeurIPS 2022 paper 写成 2021）
□ 标题（前几个词最容易被 hallucinate 改写）
□ Venue / 会议名（NeurIPS vs NIPS / EMNLP vs EMNLP Findings / arXiv vs published version）
□ 卷期号 / 页码（journal 引用）
□ DOI / arXiv ID（如果有）
□ Paper 是否真实存在（hallucinated paper 是最严重的硬伤）
□ 正文里的 cite 用得对吗（cite 了 paper A 但说的是 paper B 的结论）

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
执行方式：Main Agent 派 2-3 个 Worker 做交叉验证
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

Main Agent 调用 spawn_worker 起 2-3 个独立 Worker，每个 Worker 独立完成全部引用的核对：

- 每个 Worker 拿到完整的 .bib 文件 + 正文 \cite 位置列表
- 用 Semantic Scholar API / Google Scholar / arXiv 搜索每一条
- 独立产出一份"引用核对清单"：每条标 ✓ 或 ✗ + 错误描述

Main Agent 收到所有 Worker 的清单后做交叉比对：

- ✅ 所有 Worker 都标 OK 的 → 接受
- ⚠️ Worker 之间结论不一致的 → Main Agent 亲自再核一遍
- ❌ 多数 Worker 标错的 → 必须修

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
修复策略
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

- 作者 / 年份 / 标题错 → 改成正确的
- venue 错（如 arxiv 版本和会议版本搞混）→ 改成应该引用的版本
- Paper 不存在（hallucinated）→ 删 cite + 重新找一个真实的同主题 paper 替代，如果找不到替代且这个 claim 离开 cite 就站不住，整段重写或删
- cite 用错位置（A 引到了 B）→ 改成正确的 cite key

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
汇报内容
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

(a) 总共多少条引用？
(b) 每个 Worker 各标出多少处问题？Worker 之间一致率？
(c) Main Agent 交叉比对后，最终确认有问题的引用数量
(d) 每一处的处理方式（修了 / 删了 / 替换了哪条新引用）
(e) 修完后 .bib 重新生成 + LaTeX 编译通过 + bibtex 没 warning

### Tool result

_source line 2002 · 2026-05-26T10:53:05.972296+00:00_

1	LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding deployment, funding, and ranking decisions. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) typically fixed to single values and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting can shift accuracy by 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously and how many items suffice. We address this with Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks (2.3M records), we find that item difficulty is the largest variance component (34--38\% in multiple-choice), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in free-form), exceeding every manipulated design factor by an order of magnitude---models disagree on which items are difficult, not in overall ability. A 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol absorbs this interaction, reducing items from 552 to 178 ($G \geq 0.80$). We observe a format-associated pattern ($n = 5$, exploratory): item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68--72\%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31--41\%). We release an open-source G-theory toolkit\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/nlp_variance_gtheory-11E1/}.}.
2

_source line 2005 · 2026-05-26T11:06:25.661420+00:00_

[飞书用户 孙震 (ou_12185aa391efc8ff13eea9c848f0ab5a)]: Keywords: Generalizability theory, LLM evaluation, variance decomposition, reproducibility, benchmark design, evaluation methodology, D-study, multiple-choice, free-form generation, item analysis
Abstract:
LLM benchmarks are the primary tool for comparing language models, guiding deployment, funding, and ranking decisions. Every evaluation involves design choices (prompt template, random seed, numerical precision, answer ordering) typically fixed to single values and assumed inconsequential. Growing evidence challenges this assumption: prompt formatting can shift accuracy by 76 percentage points, and answer ordering can flip model rankings. Yet each study examines one factor in isolation, leaving unanswered which sources dominate when all vary simultaneously and how many items suffice. We address this with Generalizability theory, a psychometric framework for multi-source variance decomposition. In a factorial experiment crossing seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks (2.3M records), we find that item difficulty is the largest variance component (34–38% in multiple-choice), and the model×item interaction is the second largest (30–36% in MC, 15–17% in free-form), exceeding every manipulated design factor by an order of magnitude—models disagree on which items are difficult, not in overall ability. A 3-prompt × 3-seed protocol absorbs this interaction, reducing items from 552 to 178 (G ≥ 0.80). We observe a format-associated pattern (n = 5, exploratory): item-related variance concentrates in multiple-choice (68–72%) but disperses in free-form tasks (31–41%). We release an open-source G-theory toolkit.
Paper Type: Long
Research Area: Resources and Evaluation
Research Area Keywords: benchmarking, evaluation methodologies, reproducibility, statistical testing for evaluation, robustness, hardness of samples
Contribution Types: Model analysis & interpretability, NLP engineering experiment, Publicly available software and/or pre-trained models, Data analysis
Languages Studied: English
Reassignment Request Area Chair: This is not a resubmission
Reassignment Request Reviewers: This is not a resubmission
Preprint: no
Preprint Status: There is no non-anonymous preprint and we do not intend to release one. (this option is binding)
Preferred Venue: EMNLP
Visa Needs: yes
Consent To Share Data: yes
Consent To Share Submission Details: On behalf of all authors, we agree to the terms above to share our submission details.
A1 Limitations Section: This paper has a limitations section.
A2 Potential Risks: N/A
B Use Or Create Scientific Artifacts: Yes
B1 Cite Creators Of Artifacts: Yes
B1 Elaboration: All eight models are cited with their original technical reports in Appendix G (Grattafiori et al. 2024; Riviere et al. 2024; Jiang et al. 2023; Yang et al. 2025; Young et al. 2024; Team OLMo et al. 2025; Cai et al. 2024; Bi et al. 2024). All five benchmarks are cited at first use in §3.3 (Hendrycks et al. 2021a; Clark et al. 2018; Zellers et al. 2019; Cobbe et al. 2021; Hendrycks et al. 2021b).
B2 Discuss The License For Artifacts: No
B2 Elaboration: The paper does not explicitly discuss licenses for the benchmarks or models used. All artifacts are publicly available open-weight models and established academic benchmarks with standard research-use terms.
B3 Artifact Use Consistent With Intended Use: Yes
B3 Elaboration: §3.2–3.4 describe the evaluation protocol consistent with standard benchmark usage; the released G-theory pipeline is intended for evaluation reliability analysis as specified in §5 and the abstract.
B4 Data Contains Personally Identifying Info Or Offensive Content: N/A
B5 Documentation Of Artifacts: Yes
B5 Elaboration: §3.2 details item sampling (200 MMLU items, stratified across 10 subjects and 4 domains, CV = 3.1%); §3.3 specifies 200 items per benchmark across five benchmarks; Table 2 documents the complete design matrix; Appendix G lists all model identifiers and prompt templates.
B6 Statistics For Data: Yes
B6 Elaboration: §3.2 reports total record counts (259,200 single-model; 460,800 cross-model BF16); §3.3 reports 2,304,000 cross-benchmark records; §4 reports variance component percentages, G-coefficients, bootstrap CIs, and D-study item budgets with explicit formulas (Eq. 1–2).
C Computational Experiments: Yes
C1 Model Size And Budget: No
C1 Elaboration: The paper reports model sizes, hardware, and total inference records, but does not report total GPU-hours.
C2 Experimental Setup And Hyperparameters: Yes
C2 Elaboration: §3.2 specifies all facet levels: 3 precisions, 6 prompts, 6 seeds, 3 temperatures, 4 orderings; §3.4 specifies few-shot counts (5-shot MMLU/ARC, 4-shot MATH, 8-shot GSM8K, 10-shot HellaSwag); §3.1 specifies Henderson Method I with 1,000 bootstrap resamples. No hyperparameter search is involved as this is an analysis study.
C3 Descriptive Statistics: Yes
C3 Elaboration: All variance components are reported with bootstrap 95% CIs (Tables 3–5); D-study projections include explicit G-coefficient values at multiple item counts (Tables 7–8); §3.6 reports fixed-vs-random model sensitivity (ΔG = 0.077–0.113); §4.5 reports four-model subset stability (|ΔG| = 0.035).
C4 Parameters For Packages: Yes
C4 Elaboration: Appendix H (Software Environment) reports all key package versions: Python 3.10, PyTorch 2.1, CUDA 12.8, vLLM 0.9.2, HuggingFace Transformers 4.40, StatsModels 0.14, and SciPy 1.12. vLLM version is also specified in the evaluation protocol (§3.4). Full pinned dependencies are available in the released code repository.

[飞书用户 孙震 (ou_12185aa391efc8ff13eea9c848f0ab5a)]: research area keywords需要从这里面选

Area Keywords at ARR
Choosing the right track may be tricky for the authors, and to aid them, in this page we provide the keywords associated with ARR tracks.
This list was initially based on ACL’23 list. In April 2024 we added Language Modeling and Human-Centered NLP tracks, in May 2025 the keywords were updated based on input from EMNLP’25 PCs. Starting from March 2026 the following tracks will be used, based on input from ACL’26 and EMNLP’26 program chairs. This list is overall expected to change over time. It is not meant to be exhaustive, but it should still give some idea of what is the focus of different areas. In the future, they can be used to analyze the submission volume for different subtopics (so that we could tell which areas are growing and may need their own areas), and for paper-reviewer matching.
How does one choose the right area, given that there are so many overlaps? Here are a few examples:
- Should a question answering resource paper go to the resource area or the question answering area? Generally, if there is a task-specific area, it takes both modeling and resource papers, but if your paper is very centered on data collection methodology, you might still want to consider the resource area.
- What about a paper on multilingual generation? It could go for the generation area if the focus is on generation strategies, or to the multilingualism area - if the focus is on those languages.
- What about work in low-resource settings - is it efficiency or multilingualism? If the focus is on the training, and the low-resource setting is simulated from a well-resourced language, then we’d suggest the efficiency area. If you are contributing resources, analysis, or new solutions for languages that haven’t had that kind of thing before, consider the multilingualism area.
The current ARR areas are as follows.
- Clinical and Biomedical Applications: biomedical knowledge extraction, discovery, and text mining; biomedical question answering; clinical and biomedical language models; clinical dialogue systems; clinical and biomedical text summarisation; NLP tools for biomedical knowledge graphs, clinical decision support, clinical coding, drug adverse event discovery, mental health; phrase mining and disease association; regulatory and ethical considerations; other clinical and biomedical applications which explicitly involve natural language as a central element in the paper contribution.
- Computational Social Science and Cultural Analytics: human behavior analysis; stance detection; frame detection and analysis; hate-speech detection; misinformation detection and analysis; psycho-demographic trait prediction; emotion detection and analysis; emoji prediction and analysis; language/cultural bias analysis; sociolinguistics; NLP tools for social analysis; quantitative analyses of news and/or social media;
- Dialogue and Interactive Systems: spoken dialogue systems; evaluation and metrics; task-oriented; bias/toxicity; factuality; retrieval; knowledge augmented; commonsense reasoning; interactive storytelling; embodied agents; applications; multi-modal dialogue systems; grounded dialog; multilingual / low resource; dialogue state tracking; conversational modeling;
- Discourse and Pragmatics: anaphora resolution; coreference resolution; bridging resolution; coherence; cohesion; discourse relations; discourse parsing; dialogue; conversation; discourse and multilinguality; argument mining; communication; discourse-level inference; inter-sentential reasoning; pragmatic inference and reasoning.
- Efficient Methods for NLP: quantization; pruning; distillation; parameter-efficient-training; data-efficient training; data augmentation; LLM efficiency; NLP in resource-constrained settings;
- Ethics, Bias, and Fairness: data ethics; model alignment; model bias/fairness evaluation; model bias/unfairness mitigation; ethical considerations in NLP applications; transparency; policy and governance; reflections and critiques;
- Generation: human evaluation; automatic evaluation; multilingualism; efficient models; few-shot generation; analysis; domain adaptation; data-to-text generation; text-to-text generation; inference methods; model architectures; retrieval-augmented generation; interactive and collaborative generation;
- Human-Centered NLP and Human-AI Interaction: human-AI interaction/cooperation; human-in-the-loop; human-centered evaluation; user-centered design; value-centered design; human factors in NLP; participatory/community-based NLP; values and culture;
- Information Extraction: named entity recognition and relation extraction; event extraction; open information extraction; knowledge base construction; entity linking/disambiguation; document-level extraction; multilingual extraction; zero/few-shot extraction;
- Information Retrieval and Text Mining: passage retrieval; dense retrieval; document representation; hashing; re-ranking; pre-training; contrastive learning; retrieval for RAG;
- Interpretability and Analysis of Models for NLP: adversarial attacks/examples/training; calibration/uncertainty; counterfactual/contrastive explanations; data influence; data shortcuts/artifacts; explanation faithfulness; feature attribution; free-text/natural language explanations; hardness of samples; hierarchical & concept explanations; human-subject application-grounded evaluations; knowledge tracing/discovering/inducing; model editing; probing; robustness; topic modeling;
- Language Modeling: adversarial attacks; chain-of-thought; fine-tuning; continual learning; hallucinations; neurosymbolic approaches; pre-training; privacy; prompting; safety; scaling; security; sparse models; red teaming; retrieval-augmented language models; robustness; transfer; watermarking;
- LLM agents: tool use; function calling; multi-modal agents; multi-agent systems; planning in agents; agent communication; agent coordination and negotiation; environment interaction; agent memory; reinforcement learning in agents; LLM-based controllers; agent evaluation; grounded agents; embodied agents;
- Linguistic Theories, Cognitive Modeling, and Psycholinguistics: linguistic theories; cognitive modeling; computational psycholinguistics;
- Machine Learning for NLP: graph-based methods; knowledge-augmented methods; multi-task learning; self-supervised learning; contrastive learning; generative models; data augmentation; word embeddings; structured prediction; transfer learning / domain adaptation; representation learning; generalization; few-shot learning; reinforcement learning; optimization methods; continual learning; adversarial training; meta learning; causality; graphical models; human-in-the-loop / active learning;
- Machine Translation: automatic evaluation; biases; domain adaptation; efficient inference for MT; efficient MT training; few-shot/zero-shot MT; human evaluation; interactive MT; MT deployment and maintenance; MT theory; modeling; multilingual MT; multimodality; online adaptation for MT; parallel decoding/non-autoregressive MT; pre-training for MT; scaling; speech translation; switch-code translation; vocabulary learning;
- Multilingualism and Cross-Lingual NLP: code-switching; mixed language; multilingualism; language contact; language change; linguistic variation; cross-lingual transfer; multilingual representations; multilingual pre-training; multilingual benchmarks; multilingual evaluation; dialects and language varieties; less-resourced languages; endangered languages; indigenous languages; minoritized languages; language documentation; resources for less-resourced languages; software and tools;
- Multimodality and Language Grounding to Vision, Robotics and Beyond: vision language navigation; cross-modal pretraining; image text matching; cross-modal content generation; vision question answering; cross-modal application; cross-modal information extraction; cross-modal machine translation; automatic speech recognition; spoken language understanding; spoken language translation; spoken language grounding; speech and vision; QA via spoken queries; spoken dialog; video processing; speech technologies; multimodality;
- NLP and Code Models: language-to-code generation, code-to-language generation, other topics which explicitly involve natural language as a central element in the paper contribution.
- NLP and Symbolic Reasoning: natural language AND symbolic reasoning topics (e.g. mathematical / symbolic

_source line 2016 · 2026-05-26T12:02:21.088955+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Item difficulty is the largest variance component in LLM evaluation (34--38\% in MC), and the model$\times$item interaction is the second largest (30--36\% in MC, 15--17\% in FF), exceeding all manipulated design factors by an order of magnitude. This reveals that models disagree on which items are difficult rather than differing uniformly in overall ability. Multi-facet evaluation designs absorb this interaction: a recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed protocol achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 MMLU items versus 552 under single-condition protocols (3.1$\times$ reduction), with cross-benchmark budgets of 93--128 items. An exploratory format comparison ($n = 5$ benchmarks) suggests MC benchmarks concentrate 68--72\% in item-related components while FF benchmarks disperse to 31--41\%, motivating per-format budget calibration pending broader confirmation. We recommend three practices: adopt multi-facet designs for published results, report item-level accuracy to expose model$\times$item interaction, and calibrate item budgets per benchmark format.
5

_source line 2063 · 2026-05-26T12:24:31.825193+00:00_

1	# Generalizability Theory for LLM Evaluation Variance Decomposition
2	
3	Code and data for applying Generalizability Theory (G-theory) to decompose variance in LLM benchmark evaluations, using Henderson Method I to estimate variance components across evaluation facets (prompt template, temperature, random seed, item ordering).
4	
5	## Requirements
6	
7	- Python 3.10+
8	- GPU with CUDA support (for vLLM inference)
9	
10	```bash
11	pip install -r requirements.txt
12	```
13	
14	## Quick Start
15	
16	**1. Prepare data and design matrix**
17	
18	```bash
19	python -m src.data.sample_mmlu --output data/mmlu_items.json
20	python -m src.data.few_shot --output data/few_shot_examples.json
21	python -m src.design.generate_design --output design_matrix.csv
22	```
23	
24	**2. Run inference**
25	
26	```bash
27	python -m src.inference.run_experiment \
28	    --design design_matrix.csv \
29	    --items data/mmlu_items.json \
30	    --few-shot data/few_shot_examples.json \
31	    --output results/all_results.jsonl \
32	    --gpu-id 0
33	```
34	
35	**3. G-study: variance decomposition**
36	
37	```bash
38	python -m src.analysis.variance_decomposition \
39	    --input results/all_results.jsonl \
40	    --output results/variance_components.json \
41	    --n-bootstrap 1000
42	```
43	
44	**4. D-study: budget optimization**
45	
46	```bash
47	python -m src.analysis.d_study \
48	    --input results/variance_components.json \
49	    --output results/d_study_results.json
50	```
51	
52	The D-study calculator (`src/analysis/d_study.py`) computes generalizability coefficients for alternative designs and finds minimum replications needed to reach target reliability thresholds (G >= 0.90, 0.95, 0.99).
53	
54	## Repository Structure
55	
56	```
57	src/
58	  data/           Data loading and sampling
59	  design/         Experimental design matrix generation
60	  inference/      vLLM-based model inference
61	  analysis/       G-study, D-study, bootstrap CI, visualization
62	scripts/          Experimen
…[truncated 211 chars]
